宏观点评 20261015  
美国 9 月 CPI：风险在 油价的 Higher for 
Longer——2026 年 9 月美国 CPI 数据点评

2026 年 10 月 15 日

相关研究  
《A 股 9 月复盘与 10 月展望——缩
量调整中的结构性分化，与政策托底
的十字路口》 
2026-10-14 
《 9 月美国 CPI 前瞻： 核心环比预计
温和，仍需关注油价扰动 ——海外周
报 20261011》 
2026-10-11

[Table_Tag]    [Table_Summary] 
◼ 核心观点：9 月美国 CPI 全面超预期，核心项目是超预期的主要来源。
与 8 月少数项目的扰动相比，9 月核心商品与服务环比的反弹具有广泛
性，通胀扩散度显著走高，超级核心通胀 3 个月均年率升至 4%以上的
高位，且二手车、 房租等分项近期走势与领先指数 “脱钩” 。这一通胀黏
性背后的必要条件是美国经济增长的韧性超预期，充分条件则是油价
“Higher for Longer”的现实强化了通胀的传导。展望看，若油价持续在
高位反复， 则高油价给明年通胀带来的基数效应利好的意义将减弱。货
币政策方面，10 月 28 日美联储加息与否对 11 月 3 日的美国中期选举
尤其重要， 因此虽然地缘事件的不确定性很高， 但我们更担忧伊朗在 10
月下旬升级冲突、给特朗普施压的可能性，这将强化美联储 10 月加息
预期走高的风险。就策略而言，中期选举仍是一个重要的事件节点，中
选后各方暂时消停、 美国国会格局清晰，市场的不确定性才会消除；我
们维持此前观点：10 月的不确定性高， 对风险偏好构成持续压制， 更适
合 11 月 3 日中选结果公布后进行右侧交易。 
◼ 美国 9 月 CPI：全面超预期， 加息预期升温。美国 9 月 CPI 环比+0.65%，
预期+0.6%， 前值+0.4%； 核心CPI 环比+0.33%， 预期+0.2%， 前值+0.29%。
CPI 同比+3.74%，预期+3.6%，前值+3.40%；核心 CPI 同比+2.56%，预
期+2.5%，前值+2.45%。由于总体 CPI 环比基本符合预期，核心项目的
超预期是 9 月通胀全面超预期的主要驱动，且核心 CPI 环比是在 8 月
“异常”的高基数上延续高于预期，带动核心 CPI 环比 3 个月均值显
著走高。 数据发布后，紧货币交易强化，交易员加息定价10 月 28 日美
联储加息概率从 20%左右升至最高 50%以上，全年加息预期最高升至约
1.5 次，美股和黄金下跌，美债收益率和美元上涨。随后，数位美联储
官员发言仍释放鸽派信号，表示不急于加息，10 月加息预期回落至约
30%，美债利率回落。 
◼ 通胀结构：核心分项环比全面走高，通胀广度扩散。总体来看，核心商
品、 居住服务、 除居住核心服务等核心CPI 大类在 9 月均环比反弹， 显
示通胀黏性仍大。①核心商品方面，9 月核心商品 CPI 环比+0.25%，为
2025 年 2 月以来最高， 前值+0.11%；结构上， 全球供应链压力、 关税和
AI 相关商品涨价均是贡献来源。其中，高占比的二手车价格拉动 9.6bps，
是核心商品环比上涨的主要贡献，且与其领先指标 Manheim 二手车走
势脱钩。与关税相关的休闲商品与服装， 二者环比分别由上月的+0.02%、
0%反弹至+0.31%、+0.86%，其中休闲商品中的玩具环比涨幅突出，拉
动核心 CPI 环比 6.6bps。②居住通胀方面，9 月居住通胀环比+0.39%，
较前值 +0.26%显著 反弹， 其 中租 金和 自住 房折算 环 比分别 反弹 至
+0.25%、+0.35%的较高中枢， 酒店住宿环比保持在+2.04%的高位。从领
先指标——领先约 16 个月的房价和领先约 6—8 个月的租金来看，居住
通胀应当仍有一定的下行空间，其在本月的反弹略超预期。③超级核心
通胀方面，由于上月的电信服务等一次性扰动的消退，9 月非居住核心
服务通胀环比由前值+0.42%回落至+0.34%， 超级核心通胀 （包括居住服
务中的酒店住宿） 环比由前值+0.51%回落至+0.42%，但环比均处于较高
中枢，且 3 个月均年率分别升至 4.43%、4.48%的高位。尤其是，从结
构来看，交运、休闲服务、酒店等更广泛的项目出现了上涨，超级核心
通胀结构所体现的通胀黏性更加突出。④通胀扩散度方面，反映通胀广
度的通胀扩散指数在 9 月显著走高，环比折年增速大于过去 5 年均值的
项目占比从 17.6%提升至 24.76%，环比折年增速大于 4%的项目占比从
20%提升至 35%。 
◼ 展望与策略启示： 风险在于油价的“higher for longer”：总体来看，9 月
美国 CPI 全面超预期， 其中核心项目是超预期的主要来源。 与8 月少数
项目的扰动相比，9 月核心商品与核心服务环比的反弹具有广泛性，通
胀扩散度显著走高， 我们认为其背后的必要条件是美国经济增长的韧性

### Page 2

2 / 7 
超预期，充分条件则是油价“Higher for Longer”的现实强化了通胀的
传导， 这对美联储来说是一个不容乐观的信号。展望看，①通胀趋势方
面，若油价持续在高位反复，则我们此前看好的“高油价带来的基数效
应”意义减弱：一来是油价的回落幅度有限， 产生的基数效应更加有限；
二来是高油价的持续意味着通胀更大的扩散风险。②货币政策方面，对
于特朗普而言，10 月 28 日美联储加息与否，对 11 月 3 日的美国中期
选举尤其重要，因此，虽然地缘事件的不确定性很高，但我们更担忧上
行风险，即伊朗在中期选举前的 10 月下旬主动挑高油价、给特朗普政
府施压的可能性，这将强化美联储 10 月加息预期走高的风险。就策略
启示而言， 我们强调在此前报告中的观点， 美国中期选举仍然是一个重
要的事件节点，中选后美伊双方暂时消停、美国国会格局清晰， 市场的
不确定性才会逐步消除；10 月的不确定性高，对风险偏好构成持续压
制，因此更适合 11 月 3 日中选结果公布后进行右侧交易。 
◼ 风险提示：油价失控推高通胀； 美联储维持高利率水平时间过长， 引发
金融系统流动性危机。

免责及评级说明部分

东吴证券投资评级标准 
投资评级基于分析师对报告发布日后 6 至 12 个月内行业或公司回报潜力相对基准表现
的预期（A 股市场基准为沪深 300 指数，香港市场基准为恒生指数，美国市场基准为标普 
500 指数， 新三板基准指数为三板成指 （针对协议转让标的） 或三板做市指数 （针对做市转
让标的），北交所基准指数为北证 50 指数），具体如下： 
公司投资评级： 
买入：预期未来 6 个月个股涨跌幅相对基准在 15%以上； 
增持：预期未来 6 个月个股涨跌幅相对基准介于 5%与 15%之间； 
中性：预期未来 6 个月个股涨跌幅相对基准介于-5%与 5%之间； 
减持：预期未来 6 个月个股涨跌幅相对基准介于-15%与-5%之间； 
卖出：预期未来 6 个月个股涨跌幅相对基准在-15%以下。 
行业投资评级： 
增持： 预期未来 6 个月内，行业指数相对强于基准 5%以上； 
中性： 预期未来 6 个月内，行业指数相对基准-5%与 5%； 
减持： 预期未来 6 个月内，行业指数相对弱于基准 5%以上。 
我们在此提醒您， 不同证券研究机构采用不同的评级术语及评级标准。 我们采用的是相
对评级体系， 表示投资的相对比重建议。 投资者买入或者卖出证券的决定应当充分考虑自身
特定状况， 如具体投资目的、 财务状况以及特定需求等， 并完整理解和使用本报告内容， 不
应视本报告为做出投资决策的唯一因素。

Document 152: AI交易忧虑压垮美股，纳指跌超2%、CoreWeave暴跌12%，VIX一度升破22，布油涨破110

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI交易忧虑压垮美股，纳指跌超2%、CoreWeave暴跌12%，VIX一度升破22，布油涨破110.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 9
Status: ok

### Page 1

芯⽚指数⼋连跌，美科技七巨头指数单⽇下挫 1.6% ，本周累跌 2.87% 。存储概念指数当天
跌 6.28% 。存储芯⽚股 SK 海⼒⼠ ADR 跌超 6.5% ，闪迪跌超 6.8% ，美光科技跌超 5.6% 。光
通信公司 Coherent 跌逾 7% 。 10 年期美债收益率扭转三连降。美元指数创逾两周新低，离
岸⼈⺠币盘中再创四年新⾼。布油盘中涨超 3% ，美油七⽇连涨。
摘要：
科技股遭新⼀轮抛售， AI 投资盈利能⼒的疑虑与油价骤升形成双重压⼒，美股周五全线下挫。纳
指单⽇重挫 2% ，芯⽚股连续第⼋个交易⽇收跌，创下 2008 年以来最⻓连跌纪录。
周五，纳斯达克 100 指数收跌 2.5% ，标普 500 指数跌 1.1% 。云计算服务商 CoreWeave 单⽇暴跌
12% ，其债券收益率本周⼀度突破 15% ，信⽤⻛险攀升⾄通常预⽰财务困境的⽔平。
本周， AI 交易的核⼼⽭盾正从算⼒需求增⻓转向融资成本、资本开⽀回报和企业盈利能⼒。云服
务商 CoreWeave 债务承压，进⼀步引发市场对 AI 数据中⼼建设融资模式的审视；存储芯⽚、光

通信和数据中⼼设备等此前受益于 AI 投资热潮的股票，也成为资⾦减仓的重点。媒体指出，
CoreWeave 股价持续下跌令市场更关注 AI 交易的融资问题，未来⼏周科技巨头的财报将检验⾼
估值能否得到盈利⽀撑。
在能源层⾯，布伦特原油结算价收于每桶约 110 美元附近， WTI 原油单⽇涨幅达 3.8% ，报每桶
101.71 美元。美国两党议员联合提出法案，拟阻⽌特朗普政府与莫斯科达成的俄罗斯柴油⼊市
协议。
AI 盈利能⼒遭质疑，芯⽚股抛压持续
AI 基础设施投⼊能否产⽣⾜够回报，已成为市场新的核⼼争议。
周五美股开盘涨跌不⼀，随后卖压逐渐扩⼤。道指⾼开后早盘⼀度上涨超过 460 点、涨幅接近
0.9% ，但午盘转跌，跌幅⼀度接近 310 点、约 0.6% 。标普 500 指数微幅⾼开后转跌，午盘跌幅⼀
度接近 1.2% ；纳斯达克综合指数低开低⾛，收盘跌幅扩⼤⾄ 2.15% 。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

其他指数中，纳斯达克 100 指数跌 2.5% ，⽣物科技指数⼀度跌超 2.5% ，费城半导体指数跌超
3.5% 。芯⽚股持续⾛弱，凸显市场对 AI 投资回报和融资模式的疑虑，已经从个别公司蔓延⾄整
个产业链。
AI 产业链股票成为抛售重灾区。存储概念指数当天跌 6.28% 。存储芯⽚股SK 海⼒⼠ ADR跌超
6.5% ，闪迪跌超 6.8% ，美光科技跌超 5.6% 。
光通信公司Coherent跌逾 7% 。为 AI 数据中⼼建设投⼊巨资的甲⻣⽂盘中跌超 4% 。 AI 指数表现
远逊于剔除 AI 股的标普指数。

### Page 3

科技七巨头遭遇重挫。万得科技七巨头单⽇下挫 1.6% ，本周累跌 2.87% 。
嘉盛的 Fawad Razaqzada 表⽰：
CoreWeave 作为 AI 算⼒基础设施的早期⼊局者，⼤量依赖⾼收益债务市场为扩张融资，在当前
利率⾼企的背景下处境尤为脆弱。 Razaqzada 指出，相关⻛险已开始向信贷市场蔓延。 " 这对 AI
相关交易形成了两难困境 —— 利率⾛⾼既压低未来现⾦流的现值，同时⼜推⾼了⽀撑 AI 下⼀阶段
增⻓所需基础设施的融资成本。 "
据彭博社报道，以超⼤规模云服务商为代表的科技巨头年度资本⽀出正逼近 1 万亿美元，其中⼤
部分来⾃债务融资。彭博⾏业研究数据显⽰，尽管最⼤型科技公司多持有投资级信⽤评级，但
基⾦经理正对 CoreWeave 等垃圾级借款⽅提出越来越多的质疑。
并不是所有⼈都愿意跟着砍估值。⾼盛的 Rich Privorotsky 认为市场在局部反应过度：代币需求
仍在超预期增⻓，纳指的前瞻市盈率已跌到约 21 倍，为本轮 AI 周期启动以来最低。

美股资⾦轮动⾄防御与能源板块
本周的价格⾛势表明，市场的讨论重⼼正在转移。问题已不再是 AI 的市场空间有多⼤，⽽是实现
这⼀前景所需的巨额投⼊最终能否带来利润。

### Page 4

美股必需消费板块逆势收红，百事可乐、亿滋国际、好时同步⾛⾼，科技七巨头⾥苹果是唯⼀
收涨的⼀只，涨幅 0.43% 。
能源股是油价回升最直接的受益者。埃克森美孚当天预告炼油业务的利润将远超预期，柴油价
格处在历史⾼位正是原因，它与雪佛⻰的涨幅都排在标普 500 前列。
莫德纳是当天标普 500 ⾥跌幅最⼤的⼀只，盘中⼀度跌超 12% ，收盘跌 7.82% 。美国⻝品药品监
督管理局拒绝受理其季节性流感疫苗 mRNA-1010 的上市申请，给出的理由是三期临床试验缺少
“ 充分且严格对照 ” 的研究设计。
分化在周线层⾯看得更清楚。本周道指涨 0.68% ，纳指跌 4.48% ，两者周度表现差超过 6 个百分
点，为 2002 年 7 ⽉以来最⼤；当⽇恰逢⽉度期权到期， VIX 盘中⼀度升破 22 ，为四个⽉来⾸次。
沃什讲话未强化 10 ⽉加息预期
周五早盘， 9 ⽉进出⼝物价与⼯业产出数据公布后， 10 年期美债收益率⼀度上⾏ 3.8 个基点⾄
5.125% ；到收盘，这⼀涨幅收窄到不⾜ 1 个基点，收益率报 5.097% ，⽇内最⾼触及 5.133% 。

本周来看， 2 年期、 5 年期、 10 年期和 30 年期收益率分别下⾏ 3.8 个、 12.4 个、 13.4 个和 15.5 个基
点，⻓端明显跑赢短端，曲线⾛成⽜平形态。就⼀周⽽⾔，美国国债迎来了 2 ⽉以来最好的表
现。

### Page 5

沃尔什在曼⾕与国际货币基⾦组织总裁格奥尔基耶娃对话时拒绝表态，只说 “ 我不预判会议 ” ，并
称两次会议之间⼤体符合美联储的预期。投资者把它读成了 10 ⽉不会再动。
他还补充说，他不相信应该暗⽰ 2026 年剩余时间⾥他会采取什么⾏动，同时坚称美联储 “ 已经⾮
常明确地表⽰，我们将实现价格稳定 ” 。真正影响市场的是他评估道：
分析认为，对于⼀个在 9 ⽉份加息并预计今年还会加息⼀次的美联储来说，这番话听起来像是委
婉地暗⽰ 10 ⽉份会议上不会加息。
本周，尽管市场对 2026 年加息的预期基本持平（尽管存在波动），但对2027 年加息的预期却明
显下滑。
在两次会议之间，世界经济总体上符合我们的预期。

### Page 6

⾼盛⾸席经济学家 Jan Hatzius 团队写道，把沃尔什的表态与鲍曼的 “ 我没有紧迫感 ” 、戴利不愿
凭有限信息做决定放在⼀起看，联邦公开市场委员会将在 10 ⽉暂停，年内第⼆次加息留给 12
⽉。
⾼盛政府债券交易主管 Andrew Mullen 的说法更直⽩。市场多年来第⼀次真正⽆视⽕热的通胀数
据，避险买盘回来了， “ 如果⼀定要我说，这轮⾏情还有后劲 ” 。
美联储内部已经有⼈承认了这件事。理事沃勒本周早些时候表⽰，收益率上⾏已经在替美联储
分担⼀部分⼯作。也就是说紧缩不必完全靠加息完成，信⽤市场⾃⼰就能把⾦融条件收紧。
美元指数创逾两周新低，离岸⼈⺠币再创四年新⾼
汇市⽅⾯，美元指数周五⾛弱，势将结束此前连续四周上涨的⾛势。 ICE 美元指数（ DXY ）连续
两⽇盘中跌破 102.00 ，在周五欧股盘中跌⾄ 101.58 ，连续两⽇刷新 10 ⽉ 6 ⽇以来低位，⽇内跌近
0.5% 。
美元⾛弱推动⼈⺠币继续上⾏，离岸⼈⺠币（ CNH ）兑美元连续两⽇盘中涨破 6.69 ，美股早盘

曾逼近 6.6830 ，连续两⽇刷新 2022 年 7 ⽉以来盘中⾼位。
本周三和周四先后公布的美国 CPI 和 PPI 通胀数据⾼于预期，⼀度推动交易员增加对美联储进⼀
步加息的押注；但沃什周五的讲话没有强化 10 ⽉加息预期，叠加美债收益率此前回落，美元的
利率优势有所减弱。

### Page 7

欧元兑美元从近期低位回升，美元兑⽇元则在 157.9 附近波动。整体⽽⾔，市场正在重新权衡美
国通胀韧性、未来利率路径与 AI 投资⻛险。若科技股抛售进⼀步加剧，避险需求可能继续影响主
要货币⾛势，但美元能否恢复上涨仍取决于美国利率预期和全球⻛险偏好的变化。
布油盘中涨超 3% ，美油七连涨，⻩⾦企稳于 4100 美元上⽅
原油⽅⾯，国际油价周五盘中有所回落，但仍处⾼位。布伦特原油此前因中东供应⻛险⽽上
涨，周五盘中⼀度在 105 美元上⽅交易；美国 WTI 原油则在每桶 97 美元附近波动。
美股早盘传出美国两党众议员提出议案阻⽌特朗普的俄罗斯柴油松绑协议后，原油加速上⾏，
美股午盘之初，美国 WTI 原油曾接近 100.90 美元，⽇内涨近 3% ，布伦特原油曾逼近 109.50 美
元，⽇内涨逾 3.5% 。
市场继续关注霍尔⽊兹海峡周边紧张局势，以及相关事件对全球原油运输和供应的影响。
油价维持⾼位也令宏观市场难以完全摆脱通胀担忧。能源成本上升可能推⾼整体通胀，并使美
联储更难迅速转向宽松；但在周五的交易中，投资者对 AI 产业链融资⻛险的关注，同样主导了股

票市场的⽅向。
到周五纽约时段尾盘，现货⻩⾦报 4,168.69 美元，全天跌 0.05% ，⼏乎收平。 12 ⽉加息选项仍然
留在桌⾯上，这是压制它反弹空间的主要原因。现货⽩银报 56.85 美元，跌 0.70% 。

### Page 8

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。
本周⻩⾦和⽩银⾛势疲软。本周三，受消费者物价指数（ CPI ）公布后实际收益率飙升的影响，
⻩⾦价格跌⾄两个⽉低点，⽩银价格也暴跌约 8% ，但随着收益率回落，⾦银价格均有所回升。
本周⽐特币价格基本持平。本周三跌破 82000 美元，周四在 80000 美元附近获得⽀撑，最终维持
在 81000 美元左右。

1

In Summary  
• 
 The first 
 Invest in Germany  
Summit  
takes place in Berlin on 19 and 20 October 2026, 
asking why and how Germany can attract 
 investment. The “why”: a top
 -
tier location 
that chronically underinvests, including from abroad.  
 Germany is the world’s third
 -
largest economy (~EUR4.4trn GDP), the only G7 country with a AAA rating from all three 
major agencies, and the third most attractive investment destination in Europe in 
 a 
2026 
EY survey. It has 1,700 hidden champions in the Mittelstand  
and R&D at 3.1% of GDP. 
Foreign companies are already here  
with 
an  
inward FDI stock 
 exceed
 ing  
EUR
 1.
0
tn
. Yet 
between 2019 and 2025, total 
 gross fixed 
 investment in Germany fell 6.4%, while it rose 
6.7% in the rest of the eurozone and 20.2% in the U.S. The gap is around EUR390bn versus 
Europe and up to 
 EUR730bn versus the U.S. Foreign investors are not closing it: 
announced 
 greenfield 
 investment 
 projects fell 10% in 2025 to 548 and are 44% below 
2019, the lowest since 2009. German savers face the same problem. Households and 
corporations save more than they invest, 
 and the surplus goes abroad (net foreign assets 
of 87% of GDP) at returns about 5pp below the U.S. and close to 3pp below other 
European countries since 1975, even below domestic returns. If German savers earn more 
at home, so can international investors.  
• 
 The problem is not a lack of capital but a lack of sufficiently attractive opportunities 
to deploy it, for domestic and foreign investors alike.  
Net public investment averaged 
zero between 2002 and 2015. Years of underinvestment should have raised returns on 
new capital, but high energy and labor costs (compounded by the second
 -
highest OECD 
tax wedge), bureaucracy (up to EUR146bn of lost output pe
 r year, per ifo
 ) and skills 
shortages prevent it. Investors themselves point to Germany’s integration of industry, 
science and research, legal certainty and skilled workforce as strengths, and to energy 
costs as Europe’s main disadvantage as an investment location
 . 
Financing is also a weak 
point: corporate financing costs are higher than in France or Italy, partly due to a 
fragmented banking sector, and projects are often too small for institutional investors 
(11,000 local authorities, 800 municipal utilities).  
• 
 The equation is changing: the fiscal shift kick
 -
starts investment, and reforms and 
public risk
 -
sharing open the door to private capital, German and international.  
The 
debt
 -
brake reform lifts the deficit from 2
 -
3% to 4
-
5% of GDP until 2028, funding defense 
and infrastructure. The “Deutschlandfonds
 ” (EUR30bn of public guarantees and equity) 
aims to mobilize EUR130bn of private capital by covering risks that private investors will 
not take alone. Foreign capital is already responding: FDI inflows rose about 50% to 
EUR86bn in 2025
 . 
European sources 
 contributed more than 
 80% of the total and the UK 
was 
 the largest single investor at 31%. Domestic savings 
 could 
 add to the pool: shifting 
5% of household financial assets out of deposits would mobilize ~EUR450bn, repatriating 
10% of net foreign assets ~EUR400bn. The “Altersvorsorgedepot” (from 1 January 2027, 
~EUR150bn over five years) and the proposed “Kapitalrente
 ” (a public fund of 
~EUR560bn by 2040) would build a domestic institutional investor base, a natural co
 -
investor for international capital. But much of the Altersvorsorgedepot may flow into 
global funds unless Germany becomes more attractive.

Invest in Germany, Yes, But How?  
Turning the investment gap into Europe’s biggest opportunity

### Page 2

2

• 
 Germany’s investment gap can therefore become one of Europe’s largest investment 
opportunities, in four verticals: energy, digital infrastructure, transport and mobility, 
and defense.  
In energy, needs reach ~EUR1.3trn by 2045 (about 30% of 2025 GDP) for 
renewables, grids, storage and hydrogen. Projects are often too small for institutional 
investors (11,000 local authorities, 800 Stadtwerke), so the enablers are advisory 
support, stand
 ardized tenders and aggregation at district and county level, and low
 -
carbon FDI rose 25% across Europe in 2025. Digital infrastructure needs EUR275
 -
500bn 
by 2030 (6
 -
11% of GDP) for hardware, telecoms, software and data centers, and foreign 
capital is alre
 ady committing, for example Google, Microsoft and Nvidia. The constraint 
is grid connection and permitting, not capital, so connection approvals should be 
prioritized. Transport and mobility 
 has  
EUR166bn planned for 2025
 -
29 (EUR107bn rail, 
EUR52bn federal roads, EUR8bn waterways), with PPP 2.0 as the enabler: performance
 -
linked service
 -
level agreements and long
 -
term offtake contracts that tie returns to 
predictable cash flows. Defense financing i
 s ~EUR734bn over five years, enough to meet 
NATO’s 3.5% of GDP target for core defense by 2029, and defense FDI projects rose 84% 
across Europe in 2025, with simpler regulation and procurement as the enabler. Across 
all four, the common enablers are faster  
 permitting and procurement, grid access, 
standardized structures and performance
 -
based PPPs with long
 -
term offtake contracts. 
Success would lift potential growth and ease pressure on the social state. Failure would 
leave Germany with higher debt and littl
 e improvement in long
 -
term growth.

Germany’s chronic investment shortage despite appealing conditions  
Germany's recent economic weakness should not obscure its enduring strengths and in
 -
principle attractiveness 
for investment.  
After several years of stagnation, the country remains Europe's largest economy, one of the world's 
leading industrial and exporting nations and home to globally competitive companies, world
 -
class research 
institutions, deep pools of skilled labor and exc
 eptionally strong public institutions. For long
 -
term investors, 
Germany offers a rare combination of economic scale, technological capability, political stability and financial 
strength.  
• 
 Scale matters: Germany is the EU's largest and the world's third
 -
largest economy (~EUR4.4trn GDP) 
meaning every investment project offers access to a substantial domestic market.  
Germany is also the 
third largest exporter, with one of the world's deepest manufacturing ecosystems, strong in machinery, 
chemicals, electrical equipment, vehicles pharmaceuticals, industrial automation and precision 
engineering, carried by a broad netwo
 rk of medium
 -
sized specialist firms. The 2026 EY attractiveness 
survey ranks Germany as the third most attractive investment destination in Europe, with a narrowing gap 
towards the top spots (France and UK).  
• 
 Its AAA rating and top institutional quality provide a stable framework for investors.  
Germany is the only 
G7 country that enjoys a AAA rating from the top three agencies, indicative of comfortably solid public 
finances. It has the top Rule of Law score in the G20 and ranks among the top 20 out of 208 countries in the 
World Bank’s various G
 overnance Indicators, reflecting strong rule of law, effective institutions, democratic 
accountability and relatively low corruption. This institutional strength provides a predictable and stable 
framework for businesses.  
• 
 The “Mittelstand
 :” 1700 hidden champions beyond the 40 global DAX companies form a uniquely strong 
economic ecosystem.  
Around 25% of German exports originate from medium
 -
sized companies that are 
niche B2B world market leaders. These hidden champions are strong innovators, filing patents at ~5x the 
rate of large corporates per employee. Many are privately held and often fa
 mily
-
owned.  
• 
 Germany is an innovation powerhouse and automation leader.  
Ranked 11th out of 139 economies in the 
2025 WIPO Global Innovation Index, it hosts seven of the world's leading innovation clusters and performs 
strongly in science, patent generation and logistics, where it ranks third globally. It is the world's fourth  
most 
automated manufacturing economy, with 449 industrial robots per 10,000 manufacturing workers (vs. a 
global average of 132). Its commitment to innovation is reflected in R&D spending equivalent to 3.1% of 
GDP, placing it 10th among roughly 80 countrie
 s tracked and ahead of the OECD average of 2.7%. Foreign

### Page 3

3

investors see this similarly: they name the close integration of industry, science and research, legal certainty 
and the skilled workforce as positives of Germany.
 1 
• 
 Human capital and scientific excellence.  
German nationals have won 17 Nobel prizes in scientific fields in 
the last 30 years alone, most recently this year (for medicine on 5 October). Max Planck Society researchers 
have been involved in 31 Nobel prizes since the institution’s founding
 . Three German universities are listed 
in the global top 50 on two different major international rankings: the Technical University of Munich, LMU 
Munich, which scored a perfect 100% on industry connections, and the University of Heidelberg. The country 
al
 so has the highest share of STEM graduates in the OECD.  
• 
 Quality of life and attractiveness for global talent.  
Three German cities make the global top 10 in Mercer’s 
most recent Quality of Living ranking (Frankfurt, Munich and Duesseldorf). Moreover, Germany is not only 
among the frontrunners in tackling climate change but also ranks sixth in Yale’s Environmental 
Performance Index, reinforcing its appeal as a destination for international talent.  
Despite these structural advantages, a massive investment gap has opened. 
 A key macroeconomic fact about 
Germany is its chronic lack of investment, which holds back capital accumulation, growth, and productivity. 
Compared to the U.S., the investment gap is very large (Figure 1): while U.S. fixed investment has increased by a 
factor of 2  
compared to the beginning of the millennium, Germany’s has increased only by 
 20%
. Even compared to 
its EU peers such as France, Sweden, or even the eurozone average, however, German investment lags.  
What’s more, German investment has declined outright since the pandemic. 
 Between 2019 and 2025, aggregate 
investments in Germany declined by 
 -
6.4%, compared with +6.7% in the rest of the Eurozone and +20.2% in the U.S. 
which alone leaves an investment gap of around EUR390bn versus Europe or EUR730bn vs the U.S. Foreign 
investor
s are not closing this gap. 
 T
he number of foreign investment projects announced in Germany fell by 10% in 
2025 to 548, the lowest level since 2009.
 2 
The decline was smaller than in France (
 -
17%) and the UK (
 -
14%
) but  
compared with 2019 Germany has lost more: 
 -
44%, against 
 -
28% in France and 
 -
34% in the UK (Figure 2). Unlike in 
France and the UK, the number has fallen every year since 2019.  
Foreign companies are nevertheless well represented in Germany. The stock of inward FDI stood at 
 EUR
 1.
0
tn at the 
end of 2024.
 3 
The German investment promotion agency GTAI recorded 1,564 foreign investment projects in 2025, 
9.3% fewer than in 2024 but a smaller decline than in the EU (
 -
18.1%) and worldwide (
 -
9.5%).

The composition of investment is skewed away from productivity
 -
enhancing items.  
Germany is a laggard in capex 
related to information and communication technology (ICT 
 – 
Figure 3), with ICT
 -
related investment less than half 
the OECD average as a share of GDP. These are investment items that are crucial determinants of productivity

 th.

External shocks don’t explain investment underperformance. 
 External shocks 
 – 
 such as the 2007
 -
08 financial 
crisis, Covid
 -
19, Russia’s full
 -
scale invasion of Ukraine, or the Iran war 
 – 
no doubt played a role. Energy price shocks 
have suppressed investment in Germany’s energy
 -
intensive industrial sectors. However, g
 iven that Germany’s 
investment underperformance is chronic, and that its peers were subject to the same shocks, these shocks only offer 
a partial explanation for Germany’s lack of investment.

Figure 4: Gross investment versus gross saving in % of GDP (2024): Germany saves but does not invest.

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Bird’s eye view: plenty of saving, not enough investment. 
 Germany’s lack of investment is particularly noteworthy 
given the fact that the economy generates large saving flows (Figure 4). National income accounting identities tell 
us that the gap between saving (S) and investment (I) is an economy’s current accoun
 t (CA) balance: CA = S 
 – 
I or, 
when abstracting from income flows on foreign assets, its export surplus.
 5  
Because the identity holds for the economy as a whole and for each sector, we can trace the surplus to its origins by 
looking at the saving
 -
investment balance of households, government, and financial and non
 -
financial corporations 
(NFC), which together add  
up to the current account (Figure 5). It turns out that, in addition to households’ persistent 
surplus of saving over investment, NFC have also been exhibiting saving surpluses. The government now invests 
more than it saves, after having made positive con
 tributions to the CA balance over much of the 2010s. In turn, CA 
surpluses imply capital exports: the excess of saving over investment is simply invested abroad.  
Figure 5: Saving net of investment by sector, and current account balance (% of GDP): Households have not been 
the only savers

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

5 
Ignoring primary income on net foreign assets and international transfers, the national income identity can be written as Y =  
C + G + I + NX, 
that is, GDP equals the sum of consumption, government spending, investment, and net exports. This can be rearran
 ged to give net exports as 
the difference between saving and investment NX = Y 
 – 
(C + G) 
 – 
I = S 
– 
I since national saving is the excess of GDP over domestic demand from 
consumption and government spending S = Y 
 – 
(C + G). In fact, Germany’s primary income  
balance (income earned on net foreign assets) is 
now almost as large as net exports
 . 
See Bundesbank (2026), Germany’s balance of payments in 2025, Monthly Report, March 2026.

Large foreign assets, poor returns  
Germany’s net foreign assets are 87% of GDP
 . Germany has amassed a large amount of claims against the rest of 
the world: its net international investment position (NIIP) 
 – 
 the difference between foreign assets and foreign 
liabilities 
 – 
is about 87% of GDP (Figure 6). From a macroeconomic perspectiv
 e, capital exports can in principle be 
beneficial for a country if it earns higher returns abroad than at home: the proof of the pudding is in the eating.  
Figure 6: Germany’s Net International Investment Position (USD bn): Savings go abroad.

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Returns on foreign assets: low 
 – 
and inferior to domestic returns
 . Germany has earned a poor return on its foreign 
assets for decades. Since 1975, its average annual foreign returns were about 5 percentage points below those of 
the U.S. and close to 3 points below those of other European countries (Table 1). What’s more
 , Germany’s foreign 
asset returns were considerably lower than the returns on its domestic assets: domestic saving has been 
systematically directed to foreign assets that earn less than the investment opportunities available at home.
 6 
Table 1: Returns on foreign assets 1975-2020 by country: Germany showing subdued returns on its foreign 
investments.

Source: Hünnekes et al. (2025). 
This suggests that Germany’s current account surplus 
 – 
or its export surplus 
 – 
turns out not to be a “good deal.” 
Instead, more investment at home would be optimal from a macroeconomic perspective. But it also begs the 
question: what are the reasons for Ge
 rmany’s excess saving?

### Page 7

The 
 multiple 
 origins of chronic investment shortage  
Households are 
 risk
 -
averse.  
 Germany’s high level of saving is usually attributed to prudent, highly risk
 -
averse 
households. Indeed, German households’ saving rate out of disposable income (Figure 7) is high in international 
comparison. At the same time, it is “natural” for the house
 hold sector in aggregate to feature a surplus of saving 
over investment. Households face an uncertain income stream but wish to smooth consumption. Consequently, they 
spread resources over a lifetime through saving  
making household surpluses a common feature of most economies.

Figure 7: Household gross saving in % of disposable income (2025): German households are heavy savers.

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Together with other factors such as risk aversion, the lack of a “Kapitalmarktkultur” (“capital markets culture”) and 
financial literacy
 7
, the institutional setup of pensions provision affects household saving incentives substantially: The 
public pay
 -
as
 -
you
-
go pension system reduces the need for households to bear market risk for retirement provision. 
As a result, German households hold 37%  
of their financial assets in bank deposits (Figure 8).  
Figure 8: Asset classes as % of total gross financial assets: Bank deposits still a key saving vehicle

Sources: Eurostat, national central banks, financial supervisory authorities, financial associations and statistical 
offices, IMF, LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research. 
In turn, banks intermediate those savings in bank deposits into low
 -
risk, low
 -
return assets: corporate loans, which 
favor incumbent firms (due to established relationships) rather than new, innovative firms; and government bonds 
(due to favorable regulator
 y treatment of banks’ holdings). As a result, savings contribute little to the creation of 
risk
-
bearing capital, and available funds for startups and scaling is scarce.

Figure 5 suggests that, in addition to households, two more sectors have in recent years tended to have a surplus of 
saving over investment: the general government 
 (
federal, state, and local government together with social security 
funds
 )
, and non
 -
financial corporations.  
The lack of government investment contributed to a lack of demand and poor infrastructure. 
 Government 
surpluses of saving over investment partly reflected a lack of public investment. Between 2002 and 2015 net 
government investment (gross investment less depreciation on the public capital stock) was zero, on average 
(Figure 9). The government ba
 rely invested enough to keep the public capital stock from shrinking.  
Only since 2015 gross investment has been sufficient to keep net investment positive. At 3.5% of GDP in 2025, 
government gross investment  
is now catching up with peers. As a result of this lack of government investment, 
Germany’s infrastructure has deteriorated, which is likely to have contributed to the lack of domestic investment by 
firms.
8  
Figure 9: General government gross vs net investment in % of GDP: Improvement underway

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Corporate surpluses persist.  
Figure 5 shows that the non
 -
financial corporate (NFC) sector has exhibited a surplus 
of saving over investment since around the turn of the millennium. Corporate saving is  
simply retained earnings, i.e. 
profits kept in the firm after paying interest, taxes and dividends. These surpluses became substantial after the great 
financial crisis and have persisted since at macro
 -
significant levels (on average since 2010: 1.6 % of G
 DP; 2025 still 
1.1% of GDP). While an excess of saving over investment is “natural” for households, it is not necessarily the case for 
NFCs. Firms invest to earn  
returns and will usually borrow to invest (or issue equity). Figure 10 shows the persistent 
shortfall of NFC investment vs saving.  
Figure 10: German non-financial corporations, gross saving vs gross investment, % of GDP: The gap persists

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

There are several reasons for the NFC surplus.  
• 
 Wage moderation and strong external demand
 . Real wages in the NFC sector have lagged productivity 
for nearly two decades (Figure 11). Only recently has real compensation for employees caught up. All else 
equal, wages lagging productivity would have boosted firms’ profits. Strong external demand, i
 f not 
accompanied by a commensurate increase in investment spending, will do the same. As shown in Figure 
1
0
, gross NFC saving jumped during periods of strong global demand (2009
 -
11 and after the pandemic, 
2020
 -
22). 
• 
 Lack of domestic demand
 . Wage moderation and a lack of public investment contributed to sluggish 
domestic demand, in turn suppressing economic growth. One consequence was that it made investment in 
faster
 -
growing overseas markets more attractive.  
• 
 Tax incentives favoring retained earnings.  
German corporate tax reforms (2001, 2008) made retaining 
profits more attractive than paying dividends, lowering payout rates and raising NFC gross saving.
 9 
These 
incentives matter in Germany because a large share of corporate profits are generated by privately held 
firms. In privately held firms, owners’ personal tax incentives, rather than pure profit maximization, may 
shape payout decisions. As the additio
 nal saving was not absorbed by investment (Figure 10), it resulted in 
higher net lending.  
Figure 11: Real compensation vs real value added per hour in the non -financial corporate sector (index, 2000 = 
100): wage restraint has contributed to non-financial sector surpluses until recently

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Economic theory suggests that years of underinvestment should raise expected returns and attract capital. In 
Germany, however, a range of supply
 -
side constraints is preventing this adjustment from taking place. 
 Energy 
costs remain elevated following the loss of cheap Russian pipeline gas and the shift towards more expensive LNG 
imports, compounded by high taxes and network charges. Businesses surveyed by EY in 2026 also see the cost of 
energy as Europe’s main dis
 advantage as an investment location, although 60% of them expect Europe’s 
attractiveness to improve over the next three years.
 10 
Labor costs are also burdened by Germany's exceptionally 
high tax wedge, the second highest in the OECD. Bureaucracy further weighs on competitiveness. According to the 
ifo economic research institute, excessive administrative requirements reduce annual o
 utput by up to EUR 146bn, 
or roughly 4% of GDP.  
11 
Although Germany ranks 12th out of 45 countries for overall business attractiveness, it ranks 
second
 -
to
-
last on costs.  
12 
Other structural weaknesses are equally apparent. Demographic decline is reducing the 
supply of skilled labor, while transport, logistics and digital infrastructure bear the consequences of years of 
underinvestment. Financing conditions are less favorable  
than in France or Italy, despite lower German risk
 -
free 
rates, with the fragmented banking system likely playing a role. These long
 -
standing challenges are now being 
compounded by structural shocks. Competition from China has intensified to the point that  
Germany runs a negative 
trade balance with China even in capital goods, for decades its core strength. Meanwhile, parts of the automotive 
sector misjudged both technology and market positioning, favoring combustion engines and fuel cell technology

are being eroded by a combination of cost pressures, institutional inefficiencies and structural headwinds.

What needs to be done: Germany’s fiscal push is a first step, but private capital needs to 
follow  
Germany’s fiscal push heralds potential investment turnaround. 
 Yet at least some of the demand
 -
side reasons for 
Germany’s large surpluses of saving over investment are on the mend. The German government has put in place a 
large fiscal expansion centered on infrastructure capex and defense. 
 Following the reform of its debt brake in 2025, 
public deficits are expected to rise from around 2
 -
3% to 4
-
5% of GDP in the next years, funding hundreds of billions 
of euros in additional spending on infrastructure, defense, energy, climate mitigation & ad
 aption and digitalization
 . 
The government’s fiscal package thus promises to kill two birds with one stone when it comes to improving German 
investment perspectives. In the short run it boosts investment spending, while in the longer run it improves public 
infrastructure, spurring 
 private investment demand.  
Government money alone is not enough: attracting private capital is necessary.  
Closing Germany’s investment 
gap will require more than public funding. The scale of the country’s investment needs is such that substantial 
private capital must also be mobilized. Foreign capital is already responding to some extent. 
 B
ased on Bundesbank 
data, FDI inflows rose by about 50% to around EUR86bn in 2025, after a fall of almost 32% in 2024  
(Figure 1
 2
)
.
13 
Investors from the UK raised their investment by 284% to EUR26bn and are now the largest single source (31% of 
the total), while U.S. investment fell by 44% to EUR11.8bn. Other EU countries contributed EUR43bn, so European 
investors account for more than 
 80% of the total.  
Figure 12: Foreign direct investment inflows to Germany, EUR bn and % of GDP: Rebound in the making

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Germany's challenge is not a shortage of capital but rather directing this capital towards productive investment.  
German households hold around EUR9trn in financial assets, a large proportion of which, by international 
standards, sits in cash and bank deposits (EUR3.4trn).
 14
. Meanwhile, the country's net foreign asset position exceeds 
EUR4.0trn (87% of GDP).  Unlocking even a small portion of these savings could have a transformative impact. 
Shifting just 5% of German households' financial assets out of deposits into more pro
 ductive assets would mobilize 
roughly EUR450bn of investment capital. In addition, repatriating for example 10% of the country’s net foreign 
investments would translate to EUR400bn of additional domestic capital.  
Making Germany more attractive for private investment starts with raising expected returns.  
 Years of 
underinvestment have already increased the potential returns on capital, as scarcity raises the marginal product of 
capital. But unlocking this opportunity requires reducing regulatory barriers, expanding standardized investment 
structures and st
 rengthening the investment culture through tax incentives, pension and insurance reforms, public
 -
private partnerships standards and strategic investment funds. Bottlenecks such as permitting, procurement, deal 
structuring and regulatory friction must be re
 duced. A key enabler for all relevant investment opportunities is

“Water, water, everywhere, nor any drop to drink”: Germany’s intermediation problem.  
The issue of available 
saving versus domestic investment 
 – 
or the country’s current account surplus 
 – 
is in large part about 
 where  
Germany 
invests. A related, but separate, question is how the savings that stay domestic are used. It turns out that another 
factor holding back investment in Germany is the intermediation of existing savings. Germany, like many other EU 
countries, is par
 ticularly lacking in long
 -
term capital due to the underdevelopment of pension funds (Figure 1
 3

since public pay
 -

 15 
Key reform: towards a more funded retirement system
 . Scandinavia and the Netherlands, economies with similar 
“culture” to Germany, have much higher stock market capitalization as a share of GDP not least because households 
accumulate claims on private pensions, which in turn recycle savings into the domest
 ic equity market (Figure 1
 4
).
16 
The development of pension funds is the key reform because they are upstream of venture capital and private 
equity in the financing chain.

The pension commission’s proposal for a funded “Kapitalrente” financed by an additional 2% contribution 
starting

 .
17

 -

 -
as
 -
you
-
go system in pillar 1 and channel 
household retirement saving into equity markets at scale. According to estimates, the public fund could reach 
around EUR560bn by 2040 (8% of GDP) and become one of  
the world’s largest pension funds within two decades.
 18 
This would create a domestic institutional investor base that sits upstream from venture capital and private equity.  
As an additional pillar 3 contribution to Germany's pension system, the forthcoming “Altersvorsorgedepot” can 
also strengthen domestic investment.  
This reform, which has already been passed, will replace the Riester pension 
from 1 January 2027. Savers will be able to invest in shares, funds and ETFs without a capital guarantee
 . 
The state 
will pay up to 50 cents for every euro saved, up to a total of 360 euros, and an additional 25 cents for every euro 
saved up to a total of 1,800 euros. This results in a maximum annual subsidy of 540 euros.

How to invest in Germany: focus on key investment themes and incentives  
From underinvestment to investment opportunity 
 – 
why now is the right time to close Germany’s up to EUR730bn 
investment gap.  
Germany's investment gap presents both a problem and an opportunity. Years of underinvestment 
have created enormous modernization needs and, in principle, high marginal returns to new capital. Until now, 
structural bottlenecks prevented investors from cap
 turing those returns. The combination of fiscal expansion, reform 
and public
 -
private risk sharing could now begin to change that equation.  The key question that arises next is where 
and how to invest. The answer can be found in four key areas: energy, dig
 ital infrastructure, transport and mobility 
and defense.  
Energy transition:  
The move towards net
 -
zero emissions and greater geopolitical resilience will require substantial 
investment in renewable energy, electricity grids, storage solutions and hydrogen infrastructure. Germany will 
require around EUR1.3tn in investments for rene
 wables, grid infrastructure, storage and hydrogen by 2045, which 
is equivalent to 30% of GDP in 2025.  Financing vehicles and project pipelines are key enablers. However, with 
around 11,000 local authorities and 800 municipal utilities (“Stadtwerke”), indi
 vidual projects are often too small to 
attract institutional investors. To address this, municipalities and regions should be supported through advisory 
services, standardized tender frameworks and training programs to facilitate the structuring of investa
 ble public
 -
private partnerships. Greater participation by districts (“
 Regierungsbezirke”) and counties (“Kreise”) would facilitate 
project aggregation and create scalable investment pipelines. Foreign investors are showing interest here: 
according to EY, the number of low
 -
carbon energy FDI projects in Europe rose by 25% in 2
 025.
21 
Digital infrastructure:  
 Digitalization and digital infrastructure are critical drivers of productivity growth. It is 
estimated that a cumulative investment of between EUR275bn and 500bn will be required by 2030, equivalent to 
6
–
11% of GDP in 2025. This includes spending on compu
 ter hardware, telecommunications equipment, software 
and AI
 -
related infrastructure, such as data centers. Grid access and permitting reform are key enablers. Germany 
should therefore prioritize grid
 -
connection approvals and permitting procedures for data c
 enters and digital 
infrastructure, treating computing capacity with the same urgency as energy security. Multi
 -
year connection queues 
have become the primary constraint on Germany’s AI and digital expansion, rather than a lack of capital or demand. 
Foreign  
capital is already 
 committing: Google plans to invest EUR5.5bn in Germany until 2029, Microsoft announced

19 
Indefi (2026): The German Pension Reform and the Shape of Things to Come  
20 
In addition, the “Frühstartrente” would extend capital
 -
funded provision to children: the state pays 
 EUR
 10 monthly into a depot from age six 
until 18. 
However, the resulting macroeconomic investment volume would be lower than that of the other additional reforms. The Bundesta
 g is 
set to vote on this measure on 16 October 2026.

EY (2026), European Attractiveness Survey, June 2026. The figures for low

EUR3.2bn in 2024 and Nvidia is part of a EUR1bn data center project with Deutsche Telekom in Munich.
 22 
AI
-

FDI projects in Europe rose by 96% in 2025, and the number also went up in Germany although total FDI projects 
declined.  
Transport and mobility: 
 Modernizing transport infrastructure is another major investment priority. The Ministry of 
Transport plans to invest EUR166bn between 2025 and 2029, including EUR107bn for rail, EUR52bn for federal 
roads, and EUR8bn for waterways. A key enabler is performa
 nce
-
based concessions. A new generation of public
 -
private partnerships (PPP 2.0) should be based on performance
 -
linked service
 -
level agreements and long
 -
term 
offtake contracts that link investor returns to measurable and predictable cash flows.  
Defense: 
 German defense financing is expected to reach approximately EUR734bn over five years, enabling the 
country to meet NATO's target of spending 3.5% of GDP on core defense by 2029. Planned procurement includes 
land systems, air power, air and missile defense,  
long
 -
range strike capabilities, unmanned systems, munitions, and 
space security assets. Foreign investors are also moving in: the number of defense FDI projects in Europe rose by 
84% in 2025 to 107.
 23 
Only seven of them were in Germany, against 20 in the UK, 14 in France, 12 in Ukraine, 10 in 
Spain and nine in Poland (Figure 14).  Key enabler: simplification. As in other sectors, reducing regulatory and 
procurement complexity will be crucial to acceler
 ating investment and delivery.

What is at stake  
Germany has a rare opportunity to transform today's fiscal stimulus into a sustained investment boom. 
 If 
successful, the country could benefit significantly from a renewed capital influx. Not only would it lift growth from 
the demand side in the short run, it would also raise potential growth and boost long
 -
term growth for decades. The 
benefits would exten
 d beyond Germany 
 – 
Europe cannot afford for its largest economy to remain its weakest link.  
The gains would be economic and political. 
 Stronger growth, functioning infrastructure and rising prosperity would 
help to restore confidence in institutions, strengthening the foundations of the social state and reducing some of the 
economic drivers behind the current political polarization. Howev
 er, successful and bold reforms, as well as 
investors willing to support Germany's renewed momentum, are equally essential to achieving this outcome.  
Failure would come at a high cost. 
 While fiscal spending could provide a temporary boost, structural weaknesses 
would persist, leaving Germany with higher debt and little improvement in long
 -
term growth. The consequences 
would extend beyond the economic sphere, including continued dependenc
 e on foreign energy and technology, 
further erosion of Europe's stability and competitiveness, and continued political polarization and populism. 
Germany's investment challenge is therefore about much more than just growth. It concerns the future economic 
and political resilience of Germany and Europe as a whole.

据 Politico 报道， Anthropic 正秘密向国会游说⼀项前沿 AI 安全监管法案草案。草案规定，
仅当模型存在造成逾 10 亿美元损失或超 50 ⼈伤亡的预测性即时⻛险时，政府⽅可⼲预。草
案要求开发者开展独⽴评估与透明化测试，并由指定法院最终裁决是否暂停模型。
摘要：
Anthropic 正悄然推动⼀项由其主导起草的 AI 安全法案，试图为联邦政府⼲预前沿 AI 模型设定极
⾼⻔槛，同时将监管权⼒严格限定于商务部和联邦法院。
据路透社 17 ⽇援引 Politico 报道， Anthropic 代表已私下向国会议员游说这份草案法规。根据法案
内容，政府只有在 AI 模型⾯临可预⻅的、迫在眉睫的⻛险 —— 即可能造成超过 10 亿美元损失，
或导致 50 ⼈以上死亡或重伤时，⽅可介⼊阻⽌模型发布或使⽤。这⼀⾼⻔槛意味着，绝⼤多数
情形下 AI 模型的开发与部署将不受政府⼲预。
Anthropic 发⾔⼈向路透社表⽰，该法案所体现的提案内容，源⾃ Anthropic 今年早些时候发布

的 " 先进 AI 框架 " （ Advanced AI Framework ）。发⾔⼈同时强调，法案将赋予政府 " 阻⽌或遏制最
强⼤模型危险部署的能⼒ " ，并要求开发者对前沿 AI 模型进⾏测试、公开评估结果，并接受独⽴
第三⽅评估。商务部未就路透社的置评请求作出回应。
触发监管的条件极为严苛
根据 Politico 的报道，法案对政府介⼊条件作出严格限定。触发监管的⻛险必须源于以下三种特
定情形之⼀： AI 模型被⽤于⽣物武器的研制或扩散、模型逃脱开发者的控制，或模型引发⽹络
攻击。
这意味着，仅仅是模型可能带来的⼴泛社会影响或经济竞争扭曲，并不构成政府叫停的法定依
据。批评者可能认为，上述条件设置过于狭窄，实际上为 Anthropic 等前沿 AI 实验室的持续运营
提供了相当程度的法律屏障。
独⽴评估机构居核⼼地位，但由实验室付费
法案设计了⼀套以独⽴评估机构为核⼼的监督机制。这些评估机构须在商务部登记注册，并负
责定期对实验室及其前沿模型进⾏评估。
值得关注的是，法案允许 AI 实验室向这些评估机构⽀付费⽤，但明确规定报酬不得与评估结论挂

钩。⼀旦独⽴评估机构判定某⼀前沿模型存在 " 迫在眉睫的列举⻛险 " ，须在完成评估后 72 ⼩时内
向商务部报告。
这⼀付费安排或引发外界对评估独⽴性的质疑，尽管法案试图以 " 报酬与结论脱钩 " 的条款加以防
范。
监管权⼒⾼度集中，其他机构明确排除在外
在权⼒边界上，法案作出了明确且严格的划定。商务部可在认定⻛险后向法院申请暂停模型运
营，由哥伦⽐亚特区联邦地区法院的联邦法官作出最终裁决。
然⽽，法案同时明⽂禁⽌美国其他任何政府部⻔、机构或官员暂停 AI 模型的开发、部署或内部使
⽤。这⼀条款将监管权⼒⾼度集中于商务部和特定联邦法院，有效阻⽌了其他联邦机构 —— 包
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

括国防部、国家安全机构等 —— 对 AI 模型采取独⽴⾏动。
对投资者⽽⾔，若该法案最终⽴法，意味着前沿 AI 模型在美国市场将享有较⾼程度的运营确定
性，监管⼲预⻛险受到法律层⾯的严格约束。

Document 225: Entering the age of the older consumer As America ages, older consumers have more leisure time, significant wealth and growing economic influence. 2 min read

Source PDF: all report pdf/Entering the age of the older consumer As America ages, older consumers have more leisure time, significant wealth and growing economic influence. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 8
Status: ok

### Page 1

13023135     1

Economy 
Entering the age of the older consumer 
01 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• The older consumer is moving center stage. America's population has been aging for decades, but the shift has accelerated: the 
share aged over 60 increased by almost 10 percentage points (pp) between 1995 and 2025. And the Census Bureau projects the 
share of the population over 60 will rise another 5pp by 2055, to stand at nearly 30% of the population. 
• More free time means a different spending mix. People aged 65 and over spend around two and a half fewer hours per day 
working and roughly two hours more on leisure than the overall adult population. Bank of America data shows that older 
households allocate relatively more of their card spending to groceries and travel, and less to restaurants, gasoline, general 
merchandise and clothing. 
• Households aged over 55 held close to $140 trillion in net worth in the second quarter of 2026, giving many older consumers 
considerable capacity to spend on travel and other leisure categories. But wealth is not evenly distributed, and healthcare and 
other cost pressures remain significant for older households on tighter or fixed incomes.

Population aging is nothing new, but it is accelerating  
The US population has been growing relatively “older” for a long time. Exhibit 1 shows that the proportion of the population 
under 20 years old shrank from the mid-1960s through 2025. At the same time, the proportion of the population in older age 
groups has been rising.  
Exhibit 1: Over time the US population has steadily aged  
Proportion of US population by age range (%)

Source: Human Mortality Database; Bank of America Institute calculations

### Page 2

  I NSTI TUTE

Additionally, there has been a notable acceleration in the relative aging of the population in recent years. For example, between 
1995 and 2025, the proportion of the population aged over 60 rose by almost 10 percentage points (pp) to 25%.  
Several factors have driven this. First, fertility rates in the US, as in much of the world, have fallen sharply. This has slowed 
natural population growth and, by reducing the number of younger people relative to older cohorts, has increased the share of 
the population at older ages. Second, more recently, net inward migration to the US has slowed. Because migrants tend to be 
younger than the overall population, weaker immigration also tends to raise the average age. Finally, rising life expectancy over 
much of the post-war period has allowed more people to survive into older age, further increasing the older population's share.  
Exhibit 2: The trend towards an older composition of the population is expected to continue  
Forecast changes in the share of population by age group relative to 2025 (pp)

Source: United States Census Bureau 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Lower fertility and, more recently, weaker net migration look set to slow overall US population growth in the decades ahead. As a 
result, older Americans are likely to play an increasingly important role in the economy, both as a share of the population and, in 
many age groups, in absolute numbers. The Census Bureau projects the share of the population over 60 will rise another 5pp by 
2055, to stand at nearly 30% of the population (Exhibit 2). For businesses, understanding how the needs and spending patterns 
of older consumers differ from those of younger generations will therefore become increasingly relevant. 
Older age groups allocate their time differently  
Why does this matter? One reason is that older people tend to spend their time and money quite differently from younger adults. 
Most obviously, many work less or have retired altogether. Data from the American Time Use Survey (ATUS) shows that people 
aged 65 and over spend, on average, around two and a half fewer hours per day working than the population aged 15+ (Exhibit 
3). 
That leaves considerably more time for other activities. People aged 65 and over spend around two hours more per day on 
leisure and sports activities, with television viewing accounting for a particularly large share of this additional leisure time 
(Exhibit 4).

Older households also spend differently  
Bank of America internal data on credit and debit card spending provides insights into how these differences in older 
generations’ use of time also impact their spending. Rather than splitting our spending data by generation, as we often do, here 
we split by age bands, allowing for consistent comparisons over time.  
Exhibit 5 shows how the composition of card spending of households aged 61-75 and those older than 75 differs from that of all 
households. A few things stand out. For one, older households devote a greater share of their overall card spending to groceries 
(food and beverages). Households aged 61-75 also allocate more spending to travel (airlines and lodging). And restaurants and 
bars, gasoline, general merchandise and clothing account for a smaller share of older households’ card spending.  
These differences may seem fairly intuitive. Older households have more leisure hours, so spend relatively more on travel to 
enjoy them. Previously we found that more available time may support travel particularly around retirement, although the 
discretionary spending lift can be temporary (for more, read our publication: Paychecks to pensions: The evolution of retiree 
spending).  
Older households are also likely to have less need to update their work wardrobes, so they probably spend less on clothing.

Still, older households’ relatively lower spending on restaurants and bars is a little more curious, perhaps, given dining out could 
be one way of making use of more leisure time. Indeed, when we look at how these spending shares are evolving, we find a 
nuanced picture. While households aged 61-75 do spend relatively less on restaurants and bars than the overall population, they 
appear to be narrowing this gap. But for those older than 75 this is not the case. In our view, the pandemic may still be weighing 
on dining-out spending among this older age group (Exhibit 6).

Aging presents opportunities, but also challenges 
In our view, the aging of the US population presents important opportunities for the economy and businesses. Older consumers 
have more leisure time and businesses that cater to these demands may therefore benefit. 
Moreover, some older consumers hold considerable amounts of net wealth and have been some of the biggest beneficiaries of 
recent rises in equities. Using data from the Federal Reserve, Exhibit 8 shows that as of the second quarter of 2026 the net 
worth of those households aged 55 and over was close to $140 trillion, around three-quarters of the total. And with equity 
markets rising strongly, these age groups have seen a more than 20% increase in their net worth over the past two years, 
potentially enhancing their spending power further.  
But an important caveat is that not all older consumers are in this position. Previous Institute work found evidence consistent 
with equity-market wealth effects being concentrated toward higher-income households (read our publication: Paychecks to 
pensions: The evolution of retiree spending). And Census Bureau research has found that around 14% of Social Security 
recipients aged over 65 rely on it for over 90% of their income. For older households without a wealth cushion, recent elevated 
cost pressures, like gasoline, will likely have put them under more significant financial strain. They will also be more dependent 
on cost-of-living (COLA) increases in Social Security to underpin their spending.  
And, more broadly, outside of card spending, healthcare costs are a much larger factor in older households’ budgets (Exhibit 9). 
The evolution of these costs going forward will be a key variable for older consumers’ budgets.

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
We consider a measure of services necessity spending that includes but is not limited to groceries, utilities, and gasoline. Discretionary services 
includes but is not limited to charitable donations, leisure travel, entertainment, and professional/consumer services. Discretionary retail 
includes but is not limited to general merchandise, miscellaneous, clothing, electronics and furniture. It excludes categories like groceries and 
gasoline. Holiday spending is defined as items in which spending in the November-December period is usually at least 20% of total annual 
spending on the category.  Value and premium grocers are determined judgmentally on a proprietary analysis of merchants by equity analysts.  
Durables spending is defined as spending on electronics, building materials, auto-related spending and furniture. Premium durables spending is 
based on a selection of retailers who are judged to sell relatively higher value products. Conversely, value durables spending is based on a 
selection of retailers who are judged to sell relatively lower value products.  
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

Document 232: First Impressions_ NZ household incomes, assets and savings update - June quarter 2026 (2026-10-18)

Source PDF: all report pdf/First Impressions_ NZ household incomes, assets and savings update - June quarter 2026 (2026-10-18).pdf

### Page 1

#consumer #newzealand
First Impressions: NZ household incomes, assets and savings
update - June quarter 2026
By Satish Ranchhod
Senior Economist, Westpac NZ
10:24 October 08 2026 Share
New Zealand incomes and savings are rising, as is the value of households’ financial
assets. However, the value of housing assets remains flat.
Key takeouts from Stats NZ’s latest update on household incomes, assets and savings (June quarter
2026)

### Page 2

Household incomes
Disposable income levels in the economy grew by 5.2% in the year to June, down from 6.5% in the
year to March

Savings and wealth
Household savings have continued to rise, climbing by $2.5b over the past quarter.

Overall household wealth levels were up 1.9% over the past year. Under the surface, the make-up of
household wealth continues to shift.

Interest costs 
Overall household income levels are continuing to rise. Much of that is due to increases in
entrepreneurial earnings (which is in part due to increased earnings in the agricultural sector). Wage
and salary growth has been more limited but has picked up.
Household savings levels have been rising for three years now, with another solid rise over the past
three months. 
Overall household wealth levels have risen modestly. Although the value of households’ financial
assets is continuing to rise, housing assets have essentially shown no growth since late 2023.
Household spending on interest costs rose just slightly in the June quarter. That’s the first increase in
two years. The easing in average borrowing costs seen over the past few years has come to a close
with the RBNZ’s hiking cycle now in train.
Underpinning the rise in overall income levels have been continued increases in entrepreneurial
earnings, which are up 12% over the past year. That will include stronger earnings in the agricultural
sector, which has been boosted by the strength in commodity export prices. That lift in agricultural
earnings has been reflected in stronger economic confidence and spending in many rural regions.
We expect commodity export earnings will remain firm over the year ahead. 
In contrast, wage and salary earnings are up just 2.7% over the past year. That’s up from 1.8% in the
year to March, but it’s a fairly muted rise, especially given the continued increases in the population
and inflation of 4.1%.
June’s large increase in savings builds on the solid increases seen over the past three years.
The savings rate in the June quarter was 3.7% of disposable incomes, down just slightly from last
quarter.
The value of households’ financial assets has continued to rise and is up 4.6% over the past year. 
However, the value of housing and land assets is down 0.9% over the past year, and it has remained
essentially unchanged since late 2023. We expect housing market conditions will remain soft over
the coming year, limiting increases in overall household wealth. 
Households’ spending on interest costs rose just slightly in the June quarter, climbing by 0.3%.
While that’s a relatively small change, it is the first increase that we’ve seen in two years.

### Page 3

Related articles

Media Contact:
Satish Ranchhod, Senior Economist
P: +64 9 336 5668
M: +64 21 710 852
Over the past few years, many borrowers saw their debt servicing costs fall as they rolled off earlier
high mortgage rates and onto lower ones. That helped to support household spending.
However, the easing in average borrowing cost has now ended. Recent months have seen mortgage
rates pushing higher as the RBNZ has begun to hike the Official Cash Rate, with further hikes
expected over the coming year. 
Looking ahead, we don’t expect a significant increase in average household borrowing costs.
However, we won’t see the same support for spending that we did over the past few years.

### Page 4

◆ The economy remains on the recovery track despite weakness in some
areas
Annualized real GDP growth in the second quarter was +1.1% QoQ, with growth
staying in positive territory for three straight quarters. Although personal
consumption exhibited weakness and capital investment declined, overall growth
was lifted by foreign demand. Exports, particularly of services, increased, and the
drop in imports of Middle Eastern crude oil also helped foreign demand turn positive.

◆ Housing starts are weak
Housing starts surged 18.6% YoY in June. They have continued to rise on a YoY
basis, but the jump was mainly due to a rebound from the slump in the same month
of the previous year. The recovery in the number of starts is actually moderate,
reflecting higher interest rates and soaring construction costs.
◆ Earthquake impact will need to be watched
Damage from the earthquake that occurred in Kumamoto Prefecture at the end of

July is weighing on economic activity in the affected areas. Although moves toward
recovery can be seen, with many companies having already resumed operations,
the impact could become prolonged in the following scenarios:
The first is if damage to capital stock such as factories and roads has been severe.
Some companies have sustained damage to production facilities, making an early
resumption of operations difficult in some cases.
The second is if a downturn in tourism demand occurs. According to some media

reports, there has been an uptick in cancellations of accommodation reservations in
Kumamoto Prefecture and neighboring prefectures since the earthquake. After the
2016 Kumamoto earthquake, the decline in travel demand from Japanese visitors
was short lived, but lower demand from foreign tourists persisted for a long period.
Annualized real GDP growth was +1.1% quarter over quarter (QoQ) in Q2 2026

### Page 5

Monthly Report of Prospects for Japan's Economy August 2026
The Japan Research Institute, Limited
Exports and inbound demand remain solid despite weakness in some areas
◆ Goods exports are on the verge of picking up
Goods exports are treading water. A sharp rise in memory chip prices is among
factors pushing up export prices, resulting in record high levels of exports in value
terms recently. On the other hand, the increase in volume terms is limited. One

possible reason is that Japan's export competitiveness has declined in fields such as
semiconductors, where global demand is growing.
Looking ahead, exports are expected to pick up. Capital investment by chipmakers
is expanding worldwide, and this should lift exports of semiconductor manufacturing
equipment, an area in which Japan has strength. However, continued caution is
needed regarding the effects of prolonged turmoil in the Middle East. A global

slowdown due to elevated crude oil prices could weigh on exports.
◆ Reluctance to visit among Chinese travelers is weighing on demand for
travel to Japan
Inbound demand is currently soft. The Chinese government's advisory urging its
citizens to refrain from traveling to Japan is having an adverse impact. The number
of Chinese visitors to Japan has fallen to about half the level prior to the advisory.
Meanwhile, the number of tourists from countries other than China remains firm.

Inbound demand is expected to stay robust going forward. Continued increases in
tourists from countries other than China will remain a supporting factor. The
expectation that the yen will remain weak will also act to push up inbound
consumption. However, there are two downside risks going forward:
The first is that the Chinese government keeps the advisory in place for a
prolonged period. When China banned group travel to South Korea in 2017, tourist

numbers stayed low for two to three years.
The second is the impact of higher crude oil prices. Major airlines have embarked
on phased increases in fuel surcharges, which could weigh on inbound demand
during the summer.

### Page 6

Personal consumption is gradually recovering thanks to a favorable income environment
◆ Higher disposable incomes are supporting personal consumption
Personal consumption continues to gradually recover. While consumption of
non-durable goods remains weak, services consumption is firm, particularly in
amusement-related services. In addition, consumption of durable goods has
increased significantly. Temporary factors such as the abolition of the environmental

performance tax on automobiles (since April 2026) and the tightening of
energy-efficiency standards for air conditioners (effective April 2027) have also
contributed to this rise.
A favorable income environment can also be pointed to as a background factor
behind the recovery in personal consumption. YoY real disposable income growth has
generally been positive since mid-2024, and the pace of increase has recently
accelerated. Employee remuneration is rising, partly as a result of agreements made

during the shunto (spring wage negotiations), and net asset income is also increasing
at a faster clip as companies hike dividends in line with stock price rises.
◆ Stock price developments could be a source of concern for personal
consumption
Personal consumption is expected to continue its gradual recovery going forward. In
addition to the favorable income environment and government policy measures to
curb price increases, a recovery in consumer confidence driven by easing tensions in

the Middle East will also provide a tailwind. Recently, the Consumer Confidence Index
has been showing signs of bottoming out.
However, a reverse wealth effect stemming from falling stock prices is a potential
cause for concern. The Nikkei Stock Average, which reached a new high in June, has
been struggling to push higher since the start of July. Estimates suggest the wealth
effect is concentrated among elderly households, but in recent years working-age

households have also increased their holdings of equity-related assets, and the
positive wealth effect for them may have supported consumption to some extent. For
this reason, if stock prices were to fall significantly, there is a danger of downward
pressure on consumption across a wide range of households.

### Page 7

セン
To
◆ The unemployment rate has settled in the mid-2% range
While the employment situation is generally favorable, weakness can be seen in
some areas. The unemployment rate has remained in the mid-2% range since 2022.
The number of employed persons in non-manufacturing, where business conditions
are favorable, is holding firm, while in manufacturing the number remains at a level
below that seen before the COVID-19 pandemic.
In the background to the drop in manufacturing employment is a slump in

production activity. Manufacturing output has been shrinking due to the effects of
U.S. tariff policy, soaring raw material prices, and a decline in the international
competitiveness of Japanese firms. As a result, pressure to reduce headcounts has
been building. Even in non-manufacturing, some industries closely linked to
manufacturing, such as transportation and wholesale/retail, are also exhibiting
weakness in labor demand.
◆ The employment situation could remain fragile in the near term

Looking ahead at the employment situation in the short term, a sense of
stagnation may persist. The contraction in output in the manufacturing sector will
continue to weigh on labor demand. Although production activity has been brisker
recently, particularly in semiconductor-related industries, the employment-creation
capacity of such industries is small. The employment-inducement coefficient per unit
of new demand tends to be lower than in other sectors, meaning that the industries

lack the strength to serve as drivers of employment.
However, in the medium to long term, the unemployment rate is expected to see a
gradual decline. Demand for labor is projected to stay solid, especially in
face-to-face services such as medical/nursing care and accommodation and
eating/drinking services, but the number of people seeking employment from the
non-labor force population has declined significantly due to the expansion in elderly
and female workforce participation. With limited room for expansion in the labor

supply, the job market is expected to tighten further.

The government is curbing inflation
◆ Policy factors are pushing down the inflation rate
In June, core inflation (all items, less fresh food) was 1.6%, staying under 2%
for the fifth consecutive month. Food price inflation has slowed, and a major

reason for this is a fallback in rice prices, which soared last year. Gasoline
subsidies are also continuing to curtail energy price increases.
Inflation looks set to hover in the 2% range in the second half of 2026. Although
energy subsidies and other government measures will soften inflationary pressure
to some extent, with imported inflationary pressure building, prices are expected
to be pushed up as higher costs are passed through to selling prices. From

FY2027 onward, the reduction in the consumption tax rate for food items will
serve to keep prices down, and inflation is expected to drop into the 1% range.
◆ The Bank of Japan (BOJ) will keep its policy rate unchanged
At its July monetary policy meeting, the BOJ decided to keep its policy rate
unchanged.
Long-term interest rates remain high. This reflects concerns about inflation
resulting from the increase in oil prices and expectations of an expansion in fiscal

expenditure.
The BOJ is expected to step up the pace of interest rate hikes while closely
monitoring the impact of higher rates on the real economy and financial/capital
markets. The next rate hike is expected to be in September.
Long-term interest rates look likely to rise gradually, reflecting heightened
anticipation of rate hikes by the BOJ and increased fiscal spending by the
government. If jitters about a deterioration in fiscal discipline resulting from such

measures as energy subsidies or the consumption tax cut take hold, there is also
a risk that long-term interest rates will rise.

◆ Surging crude oil prices have become a source of upward pressure on
prices
With crude oil prices having spiked against the backdrop of the worsening situation
in the Middle East, corporate input costs have risen sharply, and pressure for price
pass-through is intensifying. Looking at the Final Demand-Intermediate Demand
price indexes, the margin of price increases at the upstream stage far exceeds that at
the downstream stage, and price pass-through pressure is also substantial at the

downstream stage.
According to estimates based on past data, the impact of rising oil prices involves
a time lag. Specifically, the push-up effect on the consumer price inflation rate
increases for around 10 months and continues to exert influence for a long period
thereafter.
◆ Price pass-through willingness is also stronger than before
Notably, food product manufacturers are planning to raise prices for a huge
number of items during the summer and beyond. According to Teikoku Databank, the

number of items slated for price increases as of the end of July, when it conducted its
latest survey, was higher than in the May and June surveys.
If government subsidies for electricity/gas bills and gasoline prices are scaled back
or ended, both energy and food prices will increase concurrently, and the consumer
price inflation rate may rise again.
Attention also needs to be paid to the possibility that price pass-through driven by
elevated oil prices will become prolonged. In the 2020s, the rate of price increases in

transactions between companies has been higher than in the 2010s, and firms'
medium-term outlooks for selling prices have also shifted upward. Events that trigger
cost increases, such as heightened geopolitical tensions, have been occurring
repeatedly, and companies may be more willing to pass through higher costs to
prices than they were before.

### Page 10

The economy is expected to continue to recover, but downside risks are present
◆ The economy is projected to stay on the recovery track
The Japanese economy should continue its gradual recovery. WTI crude oil
futures prices are expected to fall moderately going forward.
In the corporate sector, government support for investment is providing a tailwind,
and demand for investment for labor saving and digital transformation remains
robust. In addition, rising global demand for AI-related goods should support exports.

Supply chain disruptions resulting from the deterioration in the Middle East situation
will begin to ease, and corporate production activity is expected to gradually recover.
In the household sector, inflationary pressure is expected to strengthen from the
summer onward due to higher crude oil prices. In response, the government will
probably ease the inflationary pressure through measures such as energy subsidies.
While the pace of increase in personal consumption is likely to slow somewhat,

consumption is expected to remain resilient amid continued high wage growth. From
FY2027 onward, the reduction in the consumption tax rate for food items should also
support personal consumption.
◆ The Middle East situation and rising interest rates constitute risks
Real GDP growth is projected to remain solid, registering +0.8% in FY2026 and
+0.9% in FY2027.
However, if the situation in the Middle East worsens again or becomes prolonged,
there is a risk that downward pressure on the economy will intensify, mainly through

three channels: 1) weaker exports, 2) raw material supply constraints, and 3) rising
prices. In addition, if market concerns about the fiscal stance of the Takaichi
government grow, the yen could weaken further, and long-term interest rates could
rise, potentially forcing the scaling-back or scrapping of expansionary policies such
as investment support measures, energy subsidies, and lower consumption tax.

ANALYST: Tajinder Dhillon, CFA October 1, 2026 
tajinder.dhillon@lseg.com; 
THIS WEEK IN EARNINGS 
AGGREGATE ESTIMATES AND REVISIONS 
 26Q3 Y/Y earnings are expected to be 30.6%. Excluding the energy sector, the Y/Y earnings estimate is 26.8%. 
 Of the 13 companies in the S&P 500 that have reported earnings to date for 26Q3, 84.6% have reported earnings 
above analyst estimates. This compares to a long-term average of 67.7% and prior four quarter average of 81.5%. 
 26Q3 Y/Y revenue is expected to be 11.6%. Excluding the energy sector, the growth estimate is 11.2%. 
 76.9% of companies have reported 26Q3 revenue above analyst expectations. This compares to a long-term 
average of 63% and an average over the past four quarters of 76.8%. 
 For 26Q3, there have been 40 negative EPS preannouncements issued by S&P 500 corporations compared to 65 
positive EPS preannouncements. By dividing 40 by 65 the N/P ratio is 0.6 for the S&P 500 Index.  
 The forward four-quarter (26Q4– 27Q3) P/E ratio for the S&P 500 is 20.1. 
 During the week of Oct. 5, three S&P 500 companies are expected to report quarterly earnings.

There has been an increase in the share-weighted earnings for the S&P 500 since the start of the quarter (to $773.2B 
from $760.7B). Seven of the eleven sectors have experienced downward revisions to estimates.

Since Aug. 1, the materials (-9.1%) and health care (-2.2%) sectors have recorded the highest percentage decreases 
in earnings, while the energy (16.3%) sector has recorded the highest percentage increase in earnings. Overall, share-
weighted earnings for the S&P 500 have increased by 1.6% since the start of the quarter.

Since Aug. 1, the health care (-1.7B) and materials (-1.5B) sectors have recorded the highest dollar-level decreases in 
earnings, while the information technology (8.3B) sector has recorded the highest dollar-level increase in earnings. 
Overall, expected share-weighted earnings for the S&P 500 have increased by 12.5B since the start of the quarter.

13 companies in the S&P 500 Index have reported earnings for Q3 2026. Of these companies, 84.6% reported 
earnings above analyst expectations and 7.7% reported earnings below analyst expectations. In a typical quarter 
(since 1994), 67% of companies beat estimates and 20% miss estimates. Over the past four quarters, 82% of 
companies beat the estimates and 15% missed estimates. 
In aggregate, companies are reporting earnings that are 5.3% above estimates, which compares to a long-term (since 
1994) average surprise factor of 4.4% and the average surprise factor over the prior four quarters of 7.7%.
